id: toolu_01YS648Rfr7mtFtPLarKJRED
```
All 219 tests pass. Now let me analyze the concerns the author flagged.

**Concern (2) — Can the current run's halt entry be double-counted on a subsequent issue's cap check?**

The `RunLog.__post_init__` persists an empty-`entries[]` shell to disk immediately, so the current-run file IS in the glob from the very first `_resume_attempts` call. But:

- For the cap-halt path: when the cap fires for issue N, the current file contains only entries for issues 1..N-1 (a different issue's halt would have already returned), so issue N's own halt entry can't be in the file yet.
- For the not-Done halt path (post-spawn): the orchestrator returns immediately (`return 1`) after the halt, so the run terminates without a subsequent `_resume_attempts` call.
- For the multi-issue Done path: previous Done entries don't count (excluded by `final_linear_state != "Done"`).

So the author's reasoning holds — but there's a subtle corner: if a future code change adds another halt path that doesn't immediately return, this becomes a footgun. The invariant is implicit, not enforced.

**Concern (1) — Ordering of cap-halt guard, before observation surfacing.**

Looking at the orchestrator carefully:

- `issue_span.set_attribute("issue.identifier", ...)`, `issue.title`, `issue.index`, `issue.total` all fire before the cap check — good.
- `print(f"drain-cycle: picked {identifier}: {issue['title']}")` also fires before — so the operator sees the issue was picked.
- `issue_span.set_attribute("issue.repo", target_repo.name)` fires immediately after `repos.resolve(issue)` and BEFORE the cap check — good (so telemetry sees which repo).

The ordering looks deliberate. The `issue.resumed` attribute would have value `True` if it weren't a cap-halt, but on cap-halt no `worktree.ensure` is called so there's no `handle.resumed` to record. Since `issue.resumed` is set only on the spawn path (line 350), the cap-halt span never carries it — but the `err-resume-cap` slug + halt_reason text are unambiguous, so it's fine.

**Concern (3) — Race or test-coverage gap on the glob.**

No race in single-user mode. Tests pre-seed and don't drive a second concurrent run. The author hasn't tested concurrent runs against the same cycle (two drains kicked off in parallel), but that's not a supported pattern — `~/.drain-cycle/active.json` exists for that purpose. Acceptable.

**Concern (4) — Cap-halt entry shape.**

The shape matches §8/§5 sentinel paths. `exit_code=-1`, `final_linear_state` = pre-attempt state, `worktree_path` = planned path (since `ensure` was never called). One thing I notice: `started_at` and `finished_at` will be within milliseconds of each other for a cap-halt (no real work happened), making `duration_seconds ≈ 0.0`. This is fine but worth noting since the duration could surprise log readers — though it's accurate (the issue truly took no time).

**Concern (5) — Test coverage gap (malformed run-log file).**

The `_resume_attempts` helper catches `(OSError, json.JSONDecodeError)`, but there's **no test** that exercises a corrupt/malformed run-log file. The author added the exception handling but didn't pin it with a test. The exception set seems correct:
- `OSError` covers permission errors, file-not-found races (file deleted between glob and read).
- `json.JSONDecodeError` covers partial-write JSON from a SIGKILL'd run.

But what about a file that parses as JSON but has unexpected shape (e.g. `entries` is not a list, or an entry is not a dict)? The code uses `payload.get("entries", [])` (safe if `entries` is missing) but then iterates and calls `entry.get(...)` — if `entries` is `null` (explicitly), `.get()` returns `[]` ... no, `None.get("entries", [])` would fail since `payload` would need to be a dict. If JSON parses to a non-dict (e.g. a list at root), `payload.get` raises `AttributeError`. That's not caught. A file containing `[]` or `"hello"` at root would crash `_resume_attempts`. **This is an unhandled edge case.**

Similarly, if `payload["entries"]` is, say, `null` explicitly, `payload.get("entries", [])` returns `None`, and `for entry in None` raises `TypeError`. Not caught.

Also, if an `entry` is not a dict (e.g. `entries: ["string"]`), `entry.get(...)` raises `AttributeError`. Not caught.

These are extreme corruption cases (since `runlog.RunLog` always writes the shell), but the `OSError, json.JSONDecodeError` claim of "graceful skip" is incomplete for malformed-but-parseable JSON.

**Concern (6) — Doc accuracy.**

Looking at design-decisions.md §14:

> The default is 3 (one fresh + three resumes); `null` removes the cap entirely.

But the cap counts **prior** halts and refuses when `prior_halts >= cap`. With `cap=3`, the issue can have 0, 1, or 2 prior halts and proceed; on the 4th attempt (3 priors) it refuses. So with `cap=3`:
- Attempt 1 (0 priors) — proceeds (the "fresh" attempt)
- Attempt 2 (1 prior) — proceeds (resume #1)
- Attempt 3 (2 priors) — proceeds (resume #2)
- Attempt 4 (3 priors) — refused

That's **one fresh + two resumes = 3 total attempts** (not "one fresh + three resumes = 4 total"). The doc says "one fresh + three resumes" — **this is wrong by one** (off-by-one).

Let me verify with the test:

```python
# test_resume_cap_halts_before_any_state_change seeds 2 prior halts with cap=2
# Expected: refusal (2 priors >= 2). Confirmed by the assertion.
```

So `prior_halts >= cap` means a cap of N allows exactly N attempts total (counting the fresh one and any resumes). With default 3, you get 3 attempts total = 1 fresh + 2 resumes.

The design doc says "one fresh + three resumes" for default 3. That's wrong. README says "After `max_resume_attempts` halts ... the orchestrator refuses" which is technically accurate (after 3 halts = 3 priors, refuse the 4th attempt). So **the README is correct, the design-decisions.md is wrong** about "one fresh + three resumes".

Actually wait — let me re-read. The README: "After `max_resume_attempts` halts (default `3`...)" means after 3 halts have occurred, refuse the next attempt. So sequence is:
- Attempt 1 — halt #1 (1 prior after this)
- Attempt 2 — halt #2 (2 priors after this)
- Attempt 3 — halt #3 (3 priors after this)
- Attempt 4 — refused (3 priors >= 3)

That's also 3 attempts allowed, then refusal — consistent with the code. So README accurate.

The design-decisions text "default is 3 (one fresh + three resumes)" implies 4 attempts allowed (1 fresh + 3 resumes = 4). That contradicts the code. **Doc bug.**

**Concern (7) — Comment rules.**

Let me scan the new code/tests for comments referencing Linear IDs, commit SHAs, ticket IDs, or fix-history narration:

- `orchestrator.py` new code: Comments are clean — no IDs, no narration.
- `tests/test_orchestrator_resume.py`: Several comments say things like "Pre-create the preserved worktree as if a prior run halted here", "Five prior Done entries plus one prior halt." — these describe sample data setup, fine.
- Note line 222-223 of test: "the previous failure mode" — that's narrative about what the current code change addresses. **This is light fix-history narration** — borderline.
- Test docstring at line 372-374: "Without this, a successfully drained issue in a prior run would consume the resume budget of a later, unrelated re-run against the same cycle — pinning the cap to `Done` entries instead of halts." — This is rationale for why the test exists, not fix-history per se. Borderline acceptable.
- The orchestrator.py new comment at line 279-284 is descriptive of the current code, not historical. Good.

Tests use sample IDs like `ABA-STUCK`, `ABA-LATER` — these are explicitly exempt per the rule ("identifiers used as **sample data** in tests... are exempt").

The design-decisions.md doc itself uses Linear IDs (US-B) and narrative — but it's a design doc, not code, and the rules specifically state durable artefacts like design-decisions.md are appropriate places for process history. Good.

**Other observations:**

1. **`docs/limits.example.yml` opening comment**: Says "Resource guardrails for drain-cycle workers" — but `max_resume_attempts` is explicitly NOT a runtime guardrail (the in-file comment on line 25 even calls this out). The file's header is now mildly imprecise since it bundles a policy cap under the "Resource guardrails" tagline. A small word-tweak ("Resource and policy caps for drain-cycle workers") would resolve it. Minor.

2. **`docs/limits.example.yml` line 9**: "Every value must be a positive number or null — a typo (0, a negative, a word) halts startup". With `max_resume_attempts` added, this should also mention "integer-only for `max_resume_attempts`" — already disclosed in the per-key comment on line 28. Acceptable.

3. **README "Limits" section**: Says "Each worker is bounded by resource guardrails so a runaway session — or a cycle of merely expensive ones — can't drain your whole quota unattended." The README example shows `max_resume_attempts: 5` under this heading without flagging that it's a different category. The body text added does note "is integer-only (the count of times the orchestrator will respawn a halted issue across re-runs)" — but doesn't make the policy-vs-guardrail distinction. Minor — the design-decisions §14 link is right there.

4. **Halt-reason `started_at` to `finished_at`**: On cap-halt, `started_at = _now_iso()` is captured at line 251 (`started_at = _now_iso()`) at the top of `_drain_one_issue`, before `repos.resolve`. The `finished_at = _now_iso()` is captured inside the cap-halt branch. The two reflect "work began here, refused here" — so `duration_seconds` includes the time `repos.resolve` took. That's reasonable but the cap-halt is logically a no-op refusal, so durations on the order of microseconds. Negligible.

5. **Telemetry — missing `issue.repo` attribute on the cap-halt span?** It IS set at line 277 BEFORE the cap check at line 285. Good — telemetry sees the repo on a cap-halt too.

6. **Span attribute consistency**: On cap-halt the code sets `issue.final_linear_state` but not `issue.exit_code`. Compare to setup-failed (line 344) which also doesn't set `issue.exit_code`. And `err-repo-resolution` (line 272) also doesn't. So the pattern is: pre-spawn halt paths don't set `issue.exit_code`. Consistent with peers. Good.

7. **PR scope discipline**: Diff is `README.md` + `docs/design-decisions.md` + `docs/limits.example.yml` + `drain_cycle/orchestrator.py` + new test file. No drift into adjacent modules. Clean scope.

8. **One quietly missing test case**: The `test_resume_reuses_preserved_worktree_and_signals_resumed_prompt` test asserts `resumed=True` made it into `prompt.build`, but doesn't assert the actual prompt content carried the resume directive (it could regress if `prompt.build` ignored `resumed=`). The test relies on PR2 having its own coverage for that. That's fine — scope discipline says wiring tests should test wiring, not re-test PR2.

9. **The "noop_claude" stub**: Exits 0 cleanly. The orchestrator then re-fetches the Linear issue (which still says Todo) and treats this as a not-Done halt. Test verifies set_state was called with In Progress, then the revert (back to Todo) would also be in `set_state_calls` — but the assertion is `(issue["id"], "In Progress") in set_state_calls`, which uses `in` — fine. Reasonable simulation of "agent died without marking Done".

10. **One nit on `_resume_attempts`**: The function reads every file in the glob, even when later iterations after one match could short-circuit (e.g., once count exceeds cap, no need to keep reading). At single-user scale with maybe a dozen prior runs, this is trivially cheap. Not worth changing.

11. **`docs/initiative-multi-agent-collaboration.md`** in untracked files — unrelated to PR4. Note: the author should ensure that's not getting bundled.

Now let me synthesize the findings.

## Review Summary

**Verdict:** REQUEST CHANGES

**Overview:** Solid PR4 that cleanly wires the three prior building blocks (worktree.ensure, prompt.build's resumed flag, max_resume_attempts) through the orchestrator. The cap-halt ordering and the no-spawn-halt entry shape are well-reasoned and match the project's existing patterns. Two concrete issues need fixing before merge: an off-by-one in the design-decisions doc, and an incomplete malformed-runlog-tolerance claim in `_resume_attempts`.

### Required Issues

- **`docs/design-decisions.md` §14, "Bounded by `limits.max_resume_attempts`"** — Off-by-one in the doc. The text reads "The default is 3 (one fresh + three resumes)" — that implies 4 total attempts. But the code uses `prior_halts >= cap`, which allows exactly `cap` total attempts. With `cap=3`: attempts 1, 2, 3 proceed (with 0, 1, 2 prior halts respectively); the 4th attempt (3 priors) is refused. So default 3 means "one fresh + two resumes" or equivalently "three total attempts before refusal." The README ("After `max_resume_attempts` halts ... refuses") and the in-file `_resume_attempts` docstring are both consistent with the code; only the design-doc parenthetical is wrong. Fix: change to "one fresh + two resumes" or "three total attempts (one fresh + two resumes)" — and double-check the README example comment `max_resume_attempts: 5      # allow more resumes for a flaky issue` reads consistently afterwards (it does: 5 total attempts = 1 fresh + 4 resumes).

- **`drain_cycle/orchestrator.py:69-79` `_resume_attempts` — malformed-but-parseable JSON crashes the helper.** The docstring promises "Unreadable or malformed log files are skipped rather than failed: a partial-write file from a SIGKILL'd earlier run must not pin the operator out of running the cycle." The `(OSError, json.JSONDecodeError)` catch handles partial writes and read errors, but not three classes of corruption:
  - Root is not a dict (e.g. JSON `null`, `[]`, `"string"`): `payload.get` raises `AttributeError`.
  - `entries` is explicitly `null` rather than missing: `for entry in None` raises `TypeError`.
  - An `entry` is not a dict: `entry.get(...)` raises `AttributeError`.
  
  These are far-edge corruptions (your own `RunLog._persist` would never produce them), but the helper goes out of its way to be tolerant — the docstring promises graceful skip, so it should deliver it. Either broaden the except to `(OSError, json.JSONDecodeError, AttributeError, TypeError)`, or guard with `isinstance(payload, dict)` and `isinstance(payload.get("entries"), list)` and `isinstance(entry, dict)` checks. Also worth adding a test that drops a `runs/<cycle_id>-corrupt.json` containing `[]` (or invalid JSON) into the glob and confirms `_resume_attempts` returns the count from the *other* files — pinning the tolerance contract the docstring asserts.

### Suggestions

- **`docs/limits.example.yml` line 3** — "Resource guardrails for drain-cycle workers." After this PR, the file contains both runtime guardrails (the six numeric caps that produce a `Breach`) and a policy cap (`max_resume_attempts`, no `Breach`). The in-file section header on line 25 ("Resume policy (not a runtime guardrail …)") draws the line correctly, but the top-of-file tagline still says "Resource guardrails" exclusively. Consider "Resource guardrails and policy caps for drain-cycle workers" or similar, so a reader who only sees the header doesn't carry the wrong category over to the new key.

- **`tests/test_orchestrator_resume.py:154-238` `test_resume_reuses_preserved_worktree_and_signals_resumed_prompt`** — The test seeds one prior halt entry "so the orchestrator's resume-cap sees this as a re-run, not a first attempt." But the resume *behaviour* (`worktree.ensure` reuses, `prompt.build(resumed=True)`) doesn't actually depend on the cap seeing a prior run — it depends on the worktree being registered on disk. With one prior halt and `max_resume_attempts=3` (default), the cap doesn't fire either way. The seed is therefore mostly decorative. If the intent is to pin "resume signalling fires on second-and-onward attempts," consider asserting `_resume_attempts` returned 1 (e.g. by reading the helper directly), or remove the seed and rely on the on-disk worktree alone driving `resumed=True`. Not load-bearing.

- **`drain_cycle/orchestrator.py:285-308` cap-halt branch** — The branch sets `issue_span.set_attribute("issue.final_linear_state", state_name)` but not `issue.resumed`. On the spawn path you set both `issue.resumed` and `issue.final_linear_state`. Setting `issue.resumed = True` on the cap-halt span (since by definition the cap can only fire on a re-run) would make trace filters like "show me all spans where `issue.resumed=true`" surface the cap-halts too, which seems like the natural query. Nice-to-have, not load-bearing.

- **`drain_cycle/orchestrator.py:78` `payload.get("entries", [])`** — once the malformed-JSON tolerance is hardened (Required #2), this line could read more defensively as `payload.get("entries") or []` to also tolerate `entries: null` without a separate isinstance check. Minor; the isinstance approach is also fine.

### Nits

- **`docs/design-decisions.md` §14** — "**Why "resumed" is a prompt directive, not a worker flag.**" is great context for §14 but tells the story of PR2's decision, not PR4's. Since PR2 has already landed without §14 context, this paragraph belongs there only in retrospect. Consider trimming or moving when this PR4 lands — not blocking.

- **`tests/test_orchestrator_resume.py:223`** — "(the previous failure mode)" is a light fix-history narration. The project's comment rules apply primarily to code comments, and this is a test docstring, but the language is consistent with the patterns the rules push against. Trivial rewording: "before the resume path, this would have failed on `git worktree add`" → "an existing worktree would otherwise collide with `git worktree add`". Optional.

### What's Done Well

- **Cap-halt ordering is correctly defended in the design doc.** The "ensure is harmless, set_state isn't" argument in §14 is exactly the right level of granularity, and the inline comment on `orchestrator.py:279-284` echoes it concisely without paraphrasing badly. The order (resolve → cap → ensure → link → set_state → spawn) is provably the only sequence that doesn't leave residue on a refused attempt.
- **No-spawn halt entry parity with §8/§5.** Using `exit_code=-1`, the planned worktree path, and routing through `_halt_message` keeps KR1 grading and the cap counter blind to whether a halt was a refusal or a spawn-failure — exactly the schema invariant the design doc names.
- **Telemetry plumbing on the cap-halt path.** `issue.repo`, `issue.final_linear_state`, and a stable `err-resume-cap` slug all fire before the early return, so Honeycomb filters and BubbleUp on the cap-halt class will work without additional work.
- **Test substitution discipline.** Real `git init` + `git worktree add` in `_init_repo`, in-process Linear stub via `monkeypatch.setattr`, real shell script for `_CLAUDE_CMD` — same pattern as `test_orchestrator_halt.py`, no mocks where a real subprocess is cheap. The `_seed_runlog_entry` helper writes a strict subset of the on-disk schema, so the test is robust to additive schema changes (matches the §8 "Additive schema" promise).
- **Done-entries-don't-count test (`test_resume_cap_does_not_count_done_entries`).** This is a non-obvious correctness pin — five prior Done entries plus one halt against a cap of 2 — that prevents a future refactor from accidentally counting any non-Done variant (e.g. `In Review`).

### Verification Story

- Tests reviewed: yes. All four new tests + the full 219-test suite pass locally (`uv run pytest` clean). The new tests cover the headline happy path, cap-fires-before-state-change, `None` is unbounded, and Done-entries-excluded. The malformed-runlog tolerance the helper docstring claims is not under test.
- Build verified: yes — `uv run pytest` green.
- Security checked: yes. The cap check reads only the operator's own `~/.drain-cycle/runs/` glob; no user-supplied input is reflected anywhere; no new subprocess surface. JSON parsing is via stdlib `json` (no eval, no yaml). The only attack surface is "operator with write access to `~/.drain-cycle/runs/` could craft files that crash `_resume_attempts`" — same scope as Required #2, and the operator is the only user of the tool.

Two Required findings to address. The reviewer's framing of the off-by-one is one valid reading — but the field is named `max_resume_attempts` and the PR3 docstring says "how many times the orchestrator will spawn a worker for a halted issue across re-runs", which by Pythonic convention (`max_retries=N` ⇒ N additional attempts after the initial) means **the code is off-by-one, not the doc**. With `>`, `cap=3` gives 1 fresh + 3 resumes = 4 total, matching the field name. Fixing code + doc + test together.

  "old_string": "def _resume_attempts(cycle_id: str, identifier: str) -> int:\n    \"\"\"Count prior halted attempts for ``identifier`` across this cycle's run logs.\n\n    Globs ``~/.drain-cycle/runs/<cycle_id>-*.json`` and tallies entries\n    whose ``issue_identifier`` matches and whose ``final_linear_state``\n    is not ``\"Done\"`` — the same shape every halt path writes. The\n    orchestrator compares this against ``limits.max_resume_attempts``\n    before spawning, so a perma-stuck issue stops consuming attempts\n    once its budget is spent. Unreadable or malformed log files are\n    skipped rather than failed: a partial-write file from a SIGKILL'd\n    earlier run must not pin the operator out of running the cycle.\n    \"\"\"\n    count = 0\n    for path in runlog.runs_dir().glob(f\"{cycle_id}-*.json\"):\n        try:\n            payload = json.loads(path.read_text())\n        except (OSError, json.JSONDecodeError):\n            continue\n        for entry in payload.get(\"entries\", []):\n            if (\n                entry.get(\"issue_identifier\") == identifier\n                and entry.get(\"final_linear_state\") != \"Done\"\n            ):\n                count += 1\n    return count",
  "new_string": "def _resume_attempts(cycle_id: str, identifier: str) -> int:\n    \"\"\"Count prior halted attempts for ``identifier`` across this cycle's run logs.\n\n    Globs ``~/.drain-cycle/runs/<cycle_id>-*.json`` and tallies entries\n    whose ``issue_identifier`` matches and whose ``final_linear_state``\n    is not ``\"Done\"`` — the same shape every halt path writes. The\n    orchestrator compares this against ``limits.max_resume_attempts``\n    before spawning, so a perma-stuck issue stops consuming attempts\n    once its budget is spent.\n\n    Unreadable, unparseable, or shape-corrupted log files are skipped\n    rather than failed: a partial-write file from a SIGKILL'd earlier\n    run, or a file whose ``entries`` is missing/null/non-list, must\n    not pin the operator out of running the cycle. The shape checks\n    are isinstance-guarded so a JSON file that parses but doesn't\n    match :class:`RunLog`'s on-disk schema is treated as opaque rather\n    than crashing the helper.\n    \"\"\"\n    count = 0\n    for path in runlog.runs_dir().glob(f\"{cycle_id}-*.json\"):\n        try:\n            payload = json.loads(path.read_text())\n        except (OSError, json.JSONDecodeError):\n            continue\n        if not isinstance(payload, dict):\n            continue\n        entries = payload.get(\"entries\")\n        if not isinstance(entries, list):\n            continue\n        for entry in entries:\n            if not isinstance(entry, dict):\n                continue\n            if (\n                entry.get(\"issue_identifier\") == identifier\n                and entry.get(\"final_linear_state\") != \"Done\"\n            ):\n                count += 1\n    return count"
}

  "new_string": "        # Resume-attempt cap fires BEFORE any worktree manipulation or\n        # Linear state change: a no-spawn refusal must leave the issue\n        # exactly where it was so a future re-run (after the operator\n        # raises the cap, clears prior runs, or finishes the work by\n        # hand) can pick up cleanly. The cap is a *policy* knob, not a\n        # runtime guardrail — no Breach is raised; see limits.py.\n        #\n        # ``max_resume_attempts=N`` allows up to N resumes after the\n        # initial attempt (one fresh + N resumes = N+1 total halts\n        # before refusal), matching the convention of ``max_retries`` in\n        # the stdlib's urllib3/requests world. The current one holds exactly one entry: the cap-halt.\n    log_files = sorted(runs_dir.glob(f\"{_CYCLE_ID}-*.json\"))\n    assert len(log_files) == 3  # two seeded + one current\n    # The current run's file is the one not in the seeded suffixes.\n    current_payload = next(\n        json.loads(p.read_text())\n        for p in log_files\n        if p.stem not in (f\"{_CYCLE_ID}-first\", f\"{_CYCLE_ID}-second\")\n    )\n    assert len(current_payload[\"entries\"]) == 1\n    entry = current_payload[\"entries\"][0]\n    assert entry[\"issue_identifier\"] == stuck[\"identifier\"]\n    assert entry[\"final_linear_state\"] == stuck[\"state\"][\"name\"]\n    assert \"resume-attempt cap reached (2/2)\" in entry[\"halt_reason\"]\n\n    # Stderr halt line matches the run-log halt_reason exactly — the same\n    # invariant every other halt path holds.\n    stderr_lines = capsys.readouterr().err.splitlines()\n    (halt_line,) = [line for line in stderr_lines if line.startswith(\"Halt: \")]\n    assert entry[\"halt_reason\"] == halt_line\n    assert \"max_resume_attempts\" in halt_line\n\n    # Second issue never attempted.\n    assert all(\n        e[\"issue_identifier\"] != later[\"identifier\"]\n        for e in current_payload[\"entries\"]\n    )",
  "new_string": "def test_resume_cap_halts_before_any_state_change(\n    tmp_path: Path,\n    monkeypatch: pytest.MonkeyPatch,\n    capsys: pytest.CaptureFixture[str],\n) -> None:\n    \"\"\"With prior halts past the cap, the orchestrator refuses to spawn.\n\n    With ``max_resume_attempts=2`` the operator opted in to two resumes\n    after the initial attempt (three total attempts before refusal).\n    Seeding three prior halted entries puts us at the refusal boundary\n    on the fourth would-be attempt.\n\n    The refusal is a no-spawn halt: no ``set_state``, no worktree call,\n    no ``claude`` invocation. The halt entry is written so KR1 grading\n    sees the refused attempt, and the halt line names the cap so the\n    operator knows how to clear it.\n    \"\"\"\n    repo = tmp_path / \"repo\"\n    repo.mkdir()\n    _init_repo(repo)\n    monkeypatch.chdir(repo)\n    monkeypatch.setenv(\"HOME\", str(tmp_path))\n\n    stuck = _issue(\"ABA-STUCK\", sort_order=1.0)\n    later = _issue(\"ABA-LATER\", sort_order=2.0)\n\n    # Seed three prior halted entries — one fresh + two resume halts —\n    # so a fourth attempt with ``max_resume_attempts=2`` is the first\n    # one past the cap (prior_halts=3 > cap=2).\n    runs_dir = tmp_path / \".drain-cycle\" / \"runs\"\n    for suffix in (\"first\", \"second\", \"third\"):\n        _seed_runlog_entry(\n            runs_dir,\n            cycle_id=_CYCLE_ID,\n            identifier=stuck[\"identifier\"],\n            final_state=\"Todo\",\n            suffix=suffix,\n        )\n\n    set_state_calls: list[tuple[str, str]] = []\n    _patch_linear(monkeypatch, [stuck, later], set_state_calls=set_state_calls)\n\n    # Claude must never be spawned on the cap-halt path; ``set_state``\n    # must never be called either (the cap fires before either).\n    forbidden = tmp_path / \"forbidden-claude.sh\"\n    forbidden.write_text(\"#!/bin/sh\\nexit 99\\n\")\n    forbidden.chmod(0o755)\n    monkeypatch.setattr(orchestrator, \"_CLAUDE_CMD\", [str(forbidden)])\n\n    exit_code = orchestrator.run(\n        _stub_repos(repo), limits.Limits(max_resume_attempts=2)\n    )\n    assert exit_code == 1\n\n    # No set_state, no spawned worker. The cap-halt fires before either.\n    assert set_state_calls == []\n    assert not (repo / \".worktrees\" / stuck[\"identifier\"]).exists()\n\n    # Three seeded files + one fresh run-log file (the current run).\n    # The current one holds exactly one entry: the cap-halt.\n    log_files = sorted(runs_dir.glob(f\"{_CYCLE_ID}-*.json\"))\n    assert len(log_files) == 4  # three seeded + one current\n    seeded_stems = {\n        f\"{_CYCLE_ID}-first\",\n        f\"{_CYCLE_ID}-second\",\n        f\"{_CYCLE_ID}-third\",\n    }\n    current_payload = next(\n        json.loads(p.read_text())\n        for p in log_files\n        if p.stem not in seeded_stems\n    )\n    assert len(current_payload[\"entries\"]) == 1\n    entry = current_payload[\"entries\"][0]\n    assert entry[\"issue_identifier\"] == stuck[\"identifier\"]\n    assert entry[\"final_linear_state\"] == stuck[\"state\"][\"name\"]\n    assert \"resume-attempt cap reached (all 2 resumes used)\" in entry[\"halt_reason\"]\n\n    # Stderr halt line matches the run-log halt_reason exactly — the same\n    # invariant every other halt path holds.\n    stderr_lines = capsys.readouterr().err.splitlines()\n    (halt_line,) = [line for line in stderr_lines if line.startswith(\"Halt: \")]\n    assert entry[\"halt_reason\"] == halt_line\n    assert \"max_resume_attempts\" in halt_line\n\n    # Second issue never attempted.\n    assert all(\n        e[\"issue_identifier\"] != later[\"identifier\"]\n        for e in current_payload[\"entries\"]\n    )\n\n\ndef test_resume_cap_allows_attempts_up_to_the_cap(\n    tmp_path: Path,\n    monkeypatch: pytest.MonkeyPatch,\n) -> None:\n    \"\"\"The cap is exclusive: ``prior_halts == cap`` still proceeds.\n\n    With ``max_resume_attempts=2`` (two resumes after the initial),\n    seeding *two* prior halts (one fresh halt + one resume halt) still\n    allows the next attempt — it is the second resume, used at the\n    boundary. The cap fires only on the *third* resume (prior_halts=3).\n    Pinning this boundary catches the off-by-one that would otherwise\n    silently shrink the cap by one.\n    \"\"\"\n    repo = tmp_path / \"repo\"\n    repo.mkdir()\n    _init_repo(repo)\n    monkeypatch.chdir(repo)\n    monkeypatch.setenv(\"HOME\", str(tmp_path))\n\n    issue = _issue(\"ABA-STUCK\", sort_order=1.0)\n\n    runs_dir = tmp_path / \".drain-cycle\" / \"runs\"\n    for suffix in (\"first\", \"second\"):\n        _seed_runlog_entry(\n            runs_dir,\n            cycle_id=_CYCLE_ID,\n            identifier=issue[\"identifier\"],\n            final_state=\"Todo\",\n            suffix=suffix,\n        )\n\n    set_state_calls: list[tuple[str, str]] = []\n    _patch_linear(monkeypatch, [issue], set_state_calls=set_state_calls)\n\n    monkeypatch.setattr(orchestrator, \"_CLAUDE_CMD\", [str(_noop_claude(tmp_path))])\n\n    exit_code = orchestrator.run(\n        _stub_repos(repo), limits.Limits(max_resume_attempts=2)\n    )\n    # The no-op claude doesn't flip to Done, so the orchestrator halts\n    # on the not-Done branch — but the pre-spawn cap did NOT fire, which\n    # is the load-bearing assertion here. The In Progress transition\n    # proves we passed the cap check.\n    assert exit_code == 1\n    assert (issue[\"id\"], \"In Progress\") in set_state_calls"
}

The helper's docstring promises graceful skip; this test\n    pins it.\n    \"\"\"\n    repo = tmp_path / \"repo\"\n    repo.mkdir()\n    _init_repo(repo)\n    monkeypatch.chdir(repo)\n    monkeypatch.setenv(\"HOME\", str(tmp_path))\n\n    issue = _issue(\"ABA-STUCK\", sort_order=1.0)\n    runs_dir = tmp_path / \".drain-cycle\" / \"runs\"\n    runs_dir.mkdir(parents=True)\n\n    # Four flavours of corruption sharing the cycle prefix so the glob\n    # picks them up: unparseable JSON, JSON whose root is a list (not a\n    # mapping), JSON whose ``entries`` is explicitly null, and JSON whose\n    # ``entries`` contains a non-dict element. Plus one valid halt entry\n    # — the only one that should count toward ``prior_halts``.\n    (runs_dir / f\"{_CYCLE_ID}-bad-json.json\").write_text(\"{not valid json\")\n    (runs_dir / f\"{_CYCLE_ID}-list-root.json\").write_text(\"[]\")\n    (runs_dir / f\"{_CYCLE_ID}-null-entries.json\").write_text(\n        json.dumps({\"cycle_id\": _CYCLE_ID, \"entries\": None}) + \"\\n\"\n    )\n    (runs_dir / f\"{_CYCLE_ID}-bad-entry.json\").write_text(\n        json.dumps({\"cycle_id\": _CYCLE_ID, \"entries\": [\"not-a-dict\"]}) + \"\\n\"\n    )\n    _seed_runlog_entry(\n        runs_dir,\n        cycle_id=_CYCLE_ID,\n        identifier=issue[\"identifier\"],\n        final_state=\"Todo\",\n        suffix=\"valid-halt\",\n    )\n\n    set_state_calls: list[tuple[str, str]] = []\n    _patch_linear(monkeypatch, [issue], set_state_calls=set_state_calls)\n    monkeypatch.setattr(orchestrator, \"_CLAUDE_CMD\", [str(_noop_claude(tmp_path))])\n\n    # Only the one valid halt entry counts, so prior_halts=1. With\n    # max_resume_attempts=1 the cap is NOT yet exceeded (1 > 1 is false),\n    # so the run proceeds and reaches the In Progress transition. If\n    # any corrupt file crashed the helper, the cycle would fail before\n    # set_state — so the assertion below pins both \"corrupt files\n    # don't crash\" AND \"valid entries are still counted\".\n    exit_code = orchestrator.run(\n        _stub_repos(repo), limits.Limits(max_resume_attempts=1)\n    )\n    assert exit_code == 1\n    assert (issue[\"id\"], \"In Progress\") in set_state_calls\n\n\ndef test_resume_cap_does_not_count_done_entries("

{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/Users/anton/src/drain-cycle/docs/design-decisions.md",
  "old_string": "**Bounded by `limits.max_resume_attempts`.** A perma-stuck issue would otherwise consume an attempt on every re-run forever. The orchestrator counts prior halted attempts for the issue across the cycle's run-log files (entries with a non-Done `final_linear_state` matching the identifier) and refuses to spawn once the count reaches the cap. The refusal is a no-spawn halt: no Linear `set_state`, no worktree manipulation, no worker invocation; a halt entry is still written so KR1 grading sees the refused attempt and the halt line names the cap so the operator knows how to clear it (raise the cap, clear prior runs, or finish by hand). The default is 3 (one fresh + three resumes); `null` removes the cap entirely. `max_resume_attempts` is a *policy* cap, not a runtime guardrail in the §9 sense — no `Breach` is raised, the check is purely pre-spawn against the run-log history, and the field validation is integer-only (`1.5` would be incoherent on a count).",
  "new_string": "**Bounded by `limits.max_resume_attempts`.** A perma-stuck issue would otherwise consume an attempt on every re-run forever. The orchestrator counts prior halted attempts for the issue across the cycle's run-log files (entries with a non-Done `final_linear_state` matching the identifier) and refuses to spawn once that count *exceeds* the cap. The refusal is a no-spawn halt: no Linear `set_state`, no worktree manipulation, no worker invocation; a halt entry is still written so KR1 grading sees the refused attempt and the halt line names the cap so the operator knows how to clear it (raise the cap, clear prior runs, or finish by hand). The semantic follows the stdlib `urllib3` / `requests` convention for `max_retries`: `max_resume_attempts=N` allows up to N resumes *after* the initial attempt, for `N+1` total halts before refusal. The default is 3 (one fresh attempt + three resumes = four total halts before the fifth would be refused); `null` removes the cap entirely. `max_resume_attempts` is a *policy* cap, not a runtime guardrail in the §9 sense — no `Breach` is raised, the check is purely pre-spawn against the run-log history, and the field validation is integer-only (`1.5` would be incoherent on a count)."
}

After `max_resume_attempts` halts (default `3`; configurable in `~/.drain-cycle/limits.yml` — see [Limits](#limits)), the orchestrator refuses to spawn for that issue and writes a cap-halt entry, so a perma-stuck issue cannot consume budget forever. Set the cap to `null` to opt out of the cap entirely. See [`docs/design-decisions.md`](docs/design-decisions.md) §14.",
  "new_string": "A subsequent `drain-cycle` against the same cycle picks the halted issue back up automatically: the preserved worktree is reused (no `git worktree` collision, no manual cleanup), and the spawned agent is told it is resuming so it reads the committed work in the worktree before continuing. After `max_resume_attempts` resumes (default `3` — one fresh attempt plus three resume attempts, for four total halts before the next would-be try is refused; configurable in `~/.drain-cycle/limits.yml`, see [Limits](#limits)), the orchestrator refuses to spawn for that issue and writes a cap-halt entry, so a perma-stuck issue cannot consume budget forever. Set the cap to `null` to opt out of the cap entirely. See [`docs/design-decisions.md`](docs/design-decisions.md) §14."
}

```json
{
  "file_path": "/tmp/pr1-body.md",
  "content": "## What\n\nAdds a new public function `worktree.ensure(repo, identifier)` that returns a `WorktreeHandle(path, resumed)`. If a worktree is already registered at the expected path, it's reused as-is — no git command runs. Otherwise, the call delegates to the existing `worktree.add()` and `resumed=False` comes back.\n\nThe helper `_is_registered_worktree` parses `git worktree list --porcelain -z` and resolves both sides of the comparison so paths with whitespace or embedded newlines stay unambiguous and match git's own canonicalisation. A non-zero git exit is treated as \"not registered\" — `ensure` falls through to `add`, and the orchestrator's existing pre-spawn halt surfaces the real error.\n\n## Why\n\nWhen drain-cycle re-runs a cycle that previously halted on an issue, the worktree from the prior run is still on disk with the agent's committed work on it. Today the second run trips over a duplicate-branch error from `git worktree add` and refuses. This is the seam a later PR in the stack will use to step past the error and resume the work that was already started, instead of forcing a manual cleanup.\n\nThis PR is the primitive only — no orchestrator caller is switched over yet, so behaviour today is unchanged.\n\n## Notes for reviewer\n\n- **Porcelain parsing**: I'd love a second pair of eyes on `_is_registered_worktree`. I think `--porcelain -z` with `pathlib.resolve()` on both sides covers whitespace, newlines, and symlink quirks. Is there a corner case I missed — case-insensitive filesystems, trailing slashes, anything Apple-flavoured?\n- **Silencing transient git failures**: treating a non-zero git exit as \"not registered\" felt right because it preserves today's error message (the duplicate-branch error from `add()` will surface). But it does mean a flaky git invocation gets quietly papered over. Push back if you'd rather propagate.\n- **The no-op-on-git-state contract**: pinned by byte-identical `git status` / `git log` assertions around a reused call. If there's a more direct way to express \"this function must not touch anything,\" I'm open to it.\n\nEntire-Checkpoint: e51a4640ea8d\n"
}
```

{
  "file_path": "/tmp/pr2-body.md",
  "content": "## What\n\nAdds a keyword-only `resumed=False` parameter to `prompt.build()`. When `True`, a short resume directive is inserted into the preamble — after the `---` separator, before \"Execution instructions:\" — telling the agent the worktree carries prior committed work and instructing it to read `git log --oneline main..HEAD` and `git status` before continuing, rather than restarting.\n\nThe four-segment preamble ordering is preserved; `_TAIL` stays the last non-empty line. The default keeps every current call site byte-identical to today's prompt.\n\n## Why\n\nWhen a later PR in the stack reuses a preserved worktree after a halt, the spawned agent needs to know it's resuming, not starting fresh. Without the directive it would re-do work that's already committed, or fight with files it expects to be missing. This is the smallest change that lets the resumed run pick up cleanly.\n\nThis PR is the prompt change only — no caller passes `resumed=True` yet, so behaviour today is unchanged.\n\n## Notes for reviewer\n\n- **Wording of the directive**: this is the bit the agent will actually read every time we resume. I tried to keep it short and operational — \"here's the state, here's what to check first, then continue\" — but I'd take a second opinion on tone. Is it firm enough to override an agent's instinct to start over? Too prescriptive? Read the actual paragraph and tell me if it would steer *you*.\n- **Insertion point**: I picked \"after `---`, before Execution instructions:\" so the resume context lands before the agent reads the task list. If you'd put it somewhere else (e.g. at the very top, or inside the execution block), say so — this is the kind of placement decision that's cheap to change now and annoying later.\n- **Byte-identical default behaviour**: enforced by tests. Worth flagging as the contract to push back on if you spot drift in future.\n\nEntire-Checkpoint: 30f83e2e8920\n"
}
```

{
  "file_path": "/tmp/pr3-body.md",
  "content": "## What\n\nAdds a `max_resume_attempts` field to the `Limits` dataclass — default `3`, set to `null` to disable. The validator gains an `_INT_ONLY_FIELDS` set so the new key rejects non-integer floats (1.5 resumes is incoherent). The rest of the limits contract is unchanged: positive number or null, bools rejected, unknown keys rejected.\n\nThis PR is the config plumbing only — the orchestrator doesn't read the new field yet, so behaviour today is unchanged.\n\n## Why\n\nThe next PR in the stack wires this into the orchestrator: a perma-stuck issue that keeps halting on every re-run shouldn't burn tokens forever. The cap counts non-Done run-log entries for the issue across the cycle's runs, and the orchestrator refuses to spawn once the cap is reached. Splitting the config off into its own PR keeps that wiring change small and reviewable.\n\n## Notes for reviewer\n\n- **Naming and semantics — the bit I most want pushback on**: `max_resume_attempts=N` means \"N resumes allowed *after* the initial attempt,\" matching urllib3's `max_retries` convention. So default=3 means one fresh attempt plus three resumes = four total halts before the next would-be try is refused. The alternative reading (\"N total attempts including the first one\") is a real risk. I'd rather hear \"this is confusing, rename it\" now than after the stack lands. Read the docstring and the `limits.example.yml` comment and tell me if they land the way I think they do.\n- **Policy cap vs. runtime guardrail**: this one is different from the existing token/time/cost caps — it doesn't raise a `Breach` when it fires. The orchestrator just refuses to spawn. I tried to spell that out in `limits.example.yml` (\"Resume policy (not a runtime guardrail — see design-decisions.md §14)\") but if it still reads as \"another resource cap,\" that's worth saying.\n- **`_INT_ONLY_FIELDS`**: minimal addition to the validator to keep the count field integer-only. If you'd prefer a different validator shape (e.g. per-field type annotations driving the check), I'm open to it.\n\nEntire-Checkpoint: 8e7c6d5f6cc9\n"
}

{
  "file_path": "/tmp/pr4-body.md",
  "content": "## What\n\nWires the three earlier PRs in the stack together inside the orchestrator:\n\n- `worktree.add(...)` becomes `worktree.ensure(...)`, and `handle.resumed` is threaded into `prompt.build(...)`.\n- A new module-level helper `_resume_attempts(cycle_id, identifier)` counts the issue's non-Done entries across the cycle's run-log files, tolerating shape-corrupted JSON.\n- A pre-spawn cap check refuses to spawn once `limits.max_resume_attempts` is exceeded. The refusal fires **before** any worktree manipulation or Linear state change, so a refused attempt leaves the issue exactly where it was.\n- The cap-halt branch writes a halt entry to the run log, sets `issue.resumed=True` and an `err-resume-cap` slug on the issue span, and prints a one-line reason to stderr.\n\nDocs: `design-decisions.md` §14 documents the resume mechanism, the cap semantics, and the supersession of §8's \"Retry\" bullet. README and `limits.example.yml` gain a `max_resume_attempts` entry.\n\n## Why\n\nThis is the user-visible payoff for the stack: a `drain-cycle` re-run after a halt now picks the halted issue back up automatically. No manual worktree cleanup, no duplicate-branch git error, no agent restarting from scratch. The cap is the safety valve so a perma-stuck issue doesn't loop forever.\n\n## Notes for reviewer\n\n- **Cap ordering** — the load-bearing invariant: resolve repo → check cap → ensure worktree → link config → set Linear state → spawn. A refused attempt should be invisible: no state change, no side effects. If you see a path where this isn't true, that's the thing I most want flagged.\n- **Off-by-one on the cap operator**: I went with `prior_halts > limits.max_resume_attempts` (not `>=`). With default cap=3, that means halts 1–4 proceed, halt 5 is refused. Reasoning: the field reads as \"resumes allowed *after* the initial attempt.\" This was the spot where my first code-review iteration caught a doc/code mismatch — I fixed the code, not the doc, because the field name plus the urllib3 convention seemed clearer than the inverse. If you'd reverse that call, I want to hear it.\n- **`_resume_attempts` JSON tolerance**: the helper defensively skips files that are unparseable JSON, have a non-dict root, have a non-list `entries`, or contain non-dict entries. Rationale: a SIGKILL'd prior drain can leave half-written debris on disk, and I don't want the operator pinned out of a re-run by a corrupt file. There's a pinned test for it, but this is the kind of code that quietly grows hairier — push back if you'd rather have it fail loudly than skip silently.\n- **Telemetry**: cap-halts set `issue.resumed=True` on the span and emit `err-resume-cap` as the error slug. The intent is that \"cap fired\" is filterable in Honeycomb without inventing a new attribute. Let me know if a separate `issue.halt_reason=\"resume-cap\"` attribute would be more useful for your dashboards.\n- **Docs weight**: §14 in `design-decisions.md` is long because it carries the cap-semantic rationale plus the supersession of §8. If it reads as too much, I can trim. Also worth flagging: `docs/tasks/rerun-after-halt-detect-cleanly.md` has a supersession banner on disk but that directory is gitignored, so GitHub readers won't see it — the supersession link in §14 is the canonical pointer.\n\nEntire-Checkpoint: 55ec74d83384\n"
}

1	"""Thin wrapper around ``git worktree``.
2	
3	Each issue gets ``.worktrees/<issue-identifier>/`` branched off ``main`` per
4	README §3, used once, then removed on Done — or preserved on halt so a
5	later re-run can resume against the committed work (see
6	``docs/design-decisions.md`` §14).
7	
8	``git worktree`` stderr is captured and surfaced in the raised
9	``RuntimeError`` on failure. The orchestrator's pre-spawn try/except
10	threads the message into the runlog's ``halt_reason`` so the operator
11	sees git's actual diagnostic (dirty tree, branch already exists,
12	missing ``main``) rather than just a non-zero exit code.
13	"""
14	from __future__ import annotations
15	
```

1	"""Per-issue and per-cycle resource guardrails.
2	
3	A spawned ``claude -p`` worker can run away — burning tokens, wall-clock,
4	and quota with no ceiling but the outer per-issue timeout. This module
5	defines the thresholds that bound that spend and the ``Breach`` value that
6	names which one was crossed.
7	
8	**Two layers, belt and suspenders.**
9	
10	* *Native belt* — ``per_issue_cost_usd`` is handed to ``claude`` as
11	  ``--max-budget-usd`` so the session self-terminates on cost without the
12	  orchestrator having to watch it.
13	* *Orchestrator suspenders* — ``per_issue_tokens`` and ``per_issue_seconds``
14	  are enforced by the worker against the live event stream: when either is
15	  crossed the worker SIGKILLs the session's process group (see
16	  ``worker.py``). The ``cycle_*`` caps are enforced by the orchestrator
17	  between issues via ``check_cycle`` — death-by-aggregate, where every issue
18	  is individually under its cap but their sum drains the quota, is exactly
19	  what the per-issue caps cannot catch.
20	
21	Token count is the primary guardrail (a subscription user pays in tokens,
22	not dollars); cost rides alongside it.
23	
24	**On/off per guardrail.** Any threshold may be ``None`` — that guardrail is
25	off. The defaults below are all live; an operator turns one off by setting
26	its key to ``null`` in ``~/.drain-cycle/limits.yml``.
27	
28	**Load precedence.** Baked-in defaults, overlaid by ``limits.yml`` if it
29	exists: a key absent from the file keeps its default, an explicit ``null``
30	turns the guardrail off, a positive number overrides it. A present-but-
31	malformed file raises ``LimitsConfigError`` rather than silently falling
32	back to defaults — a typo that quietly disabled a cap the operator believed
33	was active is worse than a loud startup halt. The defaults are deliberately
34	generous starting points; recalibrate against real run-log spend.
35	"""
36	from __future__ import annotations
37	
38	from dataclasses import dataclass, fields
39	from pathlib import Path
40	from typing import Any
41	
42	import yaml
43	
44	_CONFIG_DISPLAY = "~/.drain-cycle/limits.yml"
45	
46	
47	class LimitsConfigError(RuntimeError):
48	    """``limits.yml`` exists but is malformed."""
49	
50	
51	@dataclass(frozen=True)
52	class Breach:
53	    """A crossed guardrail, recorded into the halting run-log entry.
54	
55	    ``scope`` is ``"per-issue"`` or ``"cycle"``; ``metric`` is ``"token"``,
56	    ``"time"``, or ``"cost"``. ``limit`` is the cap that was set and
57	    ``observed`` the value at the moment of the breach. ``describe`` renders
58	    the operator-facing line — the worker uses it for a per-issue kill and
59	    the orchestrator for a cycle stop, so the wording cannot drift between
60	    the two surfaces.
61	    """
62	
63	    scope: str
64	    metric: str
65	    limit: float
66	    observed: float
67	
68	    def describe(self) -> str:
69	        return f"{self.scope} {self.metric} cap exceeded: {self._detail()}"
70	
71	    def _detail(self) -> str:
72	        if self.metric == "cost":
73	            return f"${self.observed:,.2f} ≥ ${self.limit:,.2f}"
74	        if self.metric == "time":
75	            return f"{self.observed:,.0f}s ≥ {self.limit:,.0f}s"
76	        return f"{self.observed:,.0f} ≥ {self.limit:,.0f} tokens"
77	
78	
79	@dataclass(frozen=True)
80	class Limits:
81	    """Resource caps. ``None`` on any field turns that guardrail off.
82	
83	    Defaults: per-issue 8M tokens · 20 min · $15; cycle 30M tokens ·
84	    90 min · $60.
85	
86	    ``max_resume_attempts`` is a *policy* cap, not a runtime guardrail:
87	    it bounds how many times the orchestrator will spawn a worker for a
88	    halted issue across re-runs before refusing further attempts
89	    (default 3, integer). ``None`` removes the cap so a perma-stuck
90	    issue keeps being retried on every re-run. No ``Breach`` is raised
91	    when it fires — the cap is checked pre-spawn against the run-log
92	    history.
93	    """
94	
95	    per_issue_tokens: int | None = 8_000_000
96	    per_issue_seconds: float | None = 20 * 60
97	    per_issue_cost_usd: float | None = 15.0
98	    cycle_tokens: int | None = 30_000_000
99	    cycle_seconds: float | None = 90 * 60
100	    cycle_cost_usd: float | None = 60.0
101	    max_resume_attempts: int | None = 3
102	
103	
104	_INT_ONLY_FIELDS = frozenset({"max_resume_attempts"})
105	
106	
107	def default_config_path() -> Path:
108	    """Resolved per call so tests can redirect via ``monkeypatch.setenv("HOME", ...)``."""
109	    return Path.home() / ".drain-cycle" / "limits.yml"
110	
111	
112	def load(path: Path | None = None) -> Limits:
113	    """Read ``limits.yml`` over the baked-in defaults.
114	
115	    A missing or empty file yields the defaults unchanged — ``limits.yml``
116	    is optional. A present file maps limit names to positive numbers (or
117	    ``null`` to disable that guardrail); unknown keys, non-numeric values,
118	    booleans, and non-positive numbers each raise ``LimitsConfigError`` with
119	    a message naming the on-disk problem.
120	    """
121	    defaults = Limits()
122	    if path is None:
123	        path = default_config_path()
124	    if not path.exists():
125	        return defaults
126	    try:
127	        data = yaml.safe_load(path.read_text())
128	    except yaml.YAMLError as exc:
129	        raise LimitsConfigError(f"{_CONFIG_DISPLAY} is not valid YAML: {exc}") from None
130	    if data is None:
131	        return defaults
132	    if not isinstance(data, dict):
133	        raise LimitsConfigError(
134	            f"{_CONFIG_DISPLAY} must be a mapping of limit names to numbers"
135	        )
136	
137	    valid = [f.name for f in fields(Limits)]
138	    unknown = set(data) - set(valid)
139	    if unknown:
140	        raise LimitsConfigError(
141	            f"{_CONFIG_DISPLAY} has unknown keys: {', '.join(sorted(unknown))}; "
142	            f"valid keys: {', '.join(valid)}"
143	        )
144	
145	    values: dict[str, Any] = {}
146	    for name in valid:
147	        if name not in data:
148	            values[name] = getattr(defaults, name)
149	            continue
150	        values[name] = _coerce(name, data[name])
151	    return Limits(**values)
152	
153	
154	def _coerce(name: str, raw: Any) -> float | None:
155	    """Validate one ``limits.yml`` value: ``None`` (off) or a positive number.
156	
157	    Fields in :data:`_INT_ONLY_FIELDS` reject non-integer floats — a count
158	    like ``max_resume_attempts: 1.5`` is semantically incoherent and would
159	    otherwise be stored verbatim against an ``int | None`` field.
160	    """
161	    if raw is None:
162	        return None
163	    # ``bool`` is a subclass of ``int``; ``per_issue_tokens: true`` would
164	    # otherwise slip through as ``1`` and silently arm an absurd cap.
165	    if isinstance(raw, bool) or not isinstance(raw, (int, float)) or raw <= 0:
166	        raise LimitsConfigError(
167	            f"{_CONFIG_DISPLAY} entry {name!r}: must be a positive number "
168	            f"or null (got {raw!r})"
169	        )
170	    if name in _INT_ONLY_FIELDS and not isinstance(raw, int):
171	        raise LimitsConfigError(
172	            f"{_CONFIG_DISPLAY} entry {name!r}: must be a positive integer "
173	            f"or null (got {raw!r})"
174	        )
175	    return raw
176	
177	
178	def check_cycle(
179	    limits: Limits, *, tokens: int, cost_usd: float, seconds: float
180	) -> Breach | None:
181	    """Return the first cycle-wide cap the running totals breach, else ``None``.
182	
183	    Tokens are checked first (the primary guardrail), then cost, then
184	    wall-clock. A guardrail set to ``None`` is skipped.
185	    """
186	    if limits.cycle_tokens is not None and tokens >= limits.cycle_tokens:
187	        return Breach("cycle", "token", limits.cycle_tokens, tokens)
188	    if limits.cycle_cost_usd is not None and cost_usd >= limits.cycle_cost_usd:
189	        return Breach("cycle", "cost", limits.cycle_cost_usd, cost_usd)
190	    if limits.cycle_seconds is not None and seconds >= limits.cycle_seconds:
191	        return Breach("cycle", "time", limits.cycle_seconds, seconds)
192	    return None
193	
```

117	    ``int | None`` field."""
118	    with pytest.raises(limits.LimitsConfigError, match="positive integer or null"):

1	---
2	name: writing-clearly-and-concisely
3	description: Apply Strunk's timeless writing rules to ANY prose humans will read—documentation, commit messages, error messages, explanations, reports, or UI text. Makes your writing clearer, stronger, and more professional.
4	---
5	
6	# Writing Clearly and Concisely
7	
8	## Overview
9	
10	William Strunk Jr.'s *The Elements of Style* (1918) teaches you to write clearly and cut ruthlessly.
11	
12	**WARNING:** `elements-of-style.md` consumes ~12,000 tokens. Read it only when writing or editing prose.
13	
14	## When to Use This Skill
15	
16	Use this skill whenever you write prose for humans:
17	
18	- Documentation, README files, technical explanations
19	- Commit messages, pull request descriptions
20	- Error messages, UI copy, help text, comments
21	- Reports, summaries, or any explanation
22	- Editing to improve clarity
23	
24	**If you're writing sentences for a human to read, use this skill.**
25	
26	## Limited Context Strategy
27	
28	When context is tight:
29	1. Write your draft using judgment
30	2. Dispatch a subagent with your draft and `elements-of-style.md`
31	3. Have the subagent copyedit and return the revision
32	
33	## All Rules
34	
35	### Elementary Rules of Usage (Grammar/Punctuation)
36	1. Form possessive singular by adding 's
37	2. Use comma after each term in series except last
38	3. Enclose parenthetic expressions between commas
39	4. Comma before conjunction introducing co-ordinate clause
40	5. Don't join independent clauses by comma
41	6. Don't break sentences in two
42	7. Participial phrase at beginning refers to grammatical subject
43	
44	### Elementary Principles of Composition
45	8. One paragraph per topic
46	9. Begin paragraph with topic sentence
47	10. **Use active voice**
48	11. **Put statements in positive form**
49	12. **Use definite, specific, concrete language**
50	13. **Omit needless words**
51	14. Avoid succession of loose sentences
52	15. Express co-ordinate ideas in similar form
53	16. **Keep related words together**
54	17. Keep to one tense in summaries
55	18. **Place emphatic words at end of sentence**
56	
57	### Section V: Words and Expressions Commonly Misused
58	Alphabetical reference for usage questions
59	
60	## Bottom Line
61	
62	Writing for humans? Read `elements-of-style.md` and apply the rules. Low on tokens? Dispatch a subagent to copyedit with the guide.
63

160	
161	Halt: ABA-243 (final state: In Progress) at /Users/you/src/drain-cycle/.worktrees/ABA-243

165	
166	After the run, one JSON log per invocation is at `~/.drain-cycle/runs/<cycle-id>-<run-timestamp>.json`. On halt, the worktree at `<target-repo>/.worktrees/<issue-identifier>/` is preserved for inspection; on success it's removed.
167	
168	A subsequent `drain-cycle` against the same cycle picks the halted issue back up automatically: the preserved worktree is reused (no `git worktree` collision, no manual cleanup), and the spawned agent is told it is resuming so it reads the committed work in the worktree before continuing. After `max_resume_attempts` resumes (default `3` — one fresh attempt plus three resume attempts, for four total halts before the next would-be try is refused; configurable in `~/.drain-cycle/limits.yml`, see [Limits](#limits)), the orchestrator refuses to spawn for that issue and writes a cap-halt entry, so a perma-stuck issue cannot consume budget forever. Set the cap to `null` to opt out of the cap entirely. See [`docs/design-decisions.md`](docs/design-decisions.md) §14.
169	
170	## Recommended companion skills
171	
172	Spawned sessions use whatever skills you've installed globally. The pairing it was designed for:
173	
174	- [**`ababushkin/pde-skills`**](https://github.com/ababushkin/pde-skills) — planning + engineering skill pack. Use it to *shape* the cycle (initiative, KRs, slices) before draining it. `drain-cycle` only multiplies execution; the quality of the cycle is upstream of this tool.
```

1	# Example ~/.drain-cycle/limits.yml
2	#
3	# Resource guardrails for drain-cycle workers. This file is OPTIONAL: with no
4	# limits.yml, the baked-in defaults below are all live. Copy to
5	# ~/.drain-cycle/limits.yml and edit only the caps you want to change.
6	#
7	# Precedence: baked-in default -> this file. A key you omit keeps its default;
8	# a number overrides it; `null` turns that guardrail OFF. Every value must be a
9	# positive number or null — a typo (0, a negative, a word) halts startup rather
10	# than silently disabling a cap you believed was active.
11	#
12	# Token count is the primary guardrail (a subscription user pays in tokens);
13	# cost rides alongside via claude's own --max-budget-usd. Per-issue tokens and
14	# time are enforced by killing the worker mid-run; the cycle caps stop the run
15	# between issues when their running total is breached.
16	#
17	# Defaults (shown explicitly here — delete any line to keep its default):
18	per_issue_tokens: 8000000     # kill a worker past this many cumulative tokens
19	per_issue_seconds: 1200       # kill a worker past 20 minutes of wall-clock
20	per_issue_cost_usd: 15        # passed to claude as --max-budget-usd
21	cycle_tokens: 30000000        # stop the cycle past this many cumulative tokens
22	cycle_seconds: 5400           # stop the cycle past 90 minutes of wall-clock
23	cycle_cost_usd: 60            # stop the cycle past this much total spend
24	
25	# To disable a guardrail, set it to null, e.g.:
26	#   per_issue_cost_usd: null
27	
```

Set the cap to `null` to opt out of the cap entirely. See [`docs/design-decisions.md`](docs/design-decisions.md) §14.

All three PR heads match local. Stack pushed cleanly.

**Summary of fixes:**

- **PR #1** — `worktree.py` module docstring: removed the `per README §3` coupling; kept the `docs/design-decisions.md §14` pointer since the reviewer marked that one merely "questionable" rather than disallowed.
- **PR #3** — deleted `_INT_ONLY_FIELDS`, its branch in `_coerce`, the explanatory docstring, the `(default 3, integer)` note in the `Limits` dataclass docstring, and `test_load_max_resume_attempts_rejects_non_integer_float`. A user who writes `max_resume_attempts: 1.5` now gets that value through and the cap operator `prior_halts > 1.5` still does the right thing — the smell-of-a-comment is gone and the special-case block with it.
- **PR #4** — rewrote the README paragraph in active voice (`Re-run drain-cycle` instead of `A subsequent drain-cycle … picks the halted issue back up automatically`), cut the no-collision/no-cleanup parenthetical (the reader infers it from "reuses the preserved worktree"), collapsed the "default 3 — one fresh + three resumes for four total halts before the next would-be try is refused; configurable in `~/.drain-cycle/limits.yml`" into "(default `3`: one fresh attempt plus three resumes)", and dropped the `docs/design-decisions.md §14` link. Also removed the now-false `Integer-only;` claim from `docs/limits.example.yml` since the int-only validation was deleted in PR3.

All 220 tests pass on the top of the stack. Awaiting your next review.

> AGENT

I'll do step 1, then move into Linear to check, then come back with a plan for anything not covered.

## Step 1 — What was different on this run

The single explicit theme: **multi-layer stacked-PR delivery** rather than the AGENTS.md default of push-direct-to-main. That brought a cluster of sub-behaviours that don't have a written home yet:

1. **Graphite stack as the delivery shape.** 4 layers, each `gt submit`, each its own PR. `gt restack` to propagate amendments upstream when a lower layer changed.
2. **Per-layer "stop and wait for re-review."** The completion ritual was split: `/code-review-and-quality` + commit + push happen per layer; the Linear issue moves to Done once the **whole stack** merges, not per layer.
3. **Owner-authorised exception to the push-direct-to-main default.** A standing instruction overrode AGENTS.md for the duration of one issue. There's no documented mechanism for that — it was held verbally and re-asserted across sessions.
4. **Cross-layer review-comment triage.** Reviewer left comments against PRs 1, 3, and 4 simultaneously; I had to apply them bottom-up with amends + `gt restack` between layers so the stack stayed coherent. There is no skill or runbook for this.
5. **PR-description iteration as a deliverable.** We rewrote each PR body multiple times — those drafts are workflow artefacts in their own right, not a side-effect of the code change.
6. **Strunk applied to prose deliverables.** The `/writing-clearly-and-consisely` skill was invoked against README paragraphs *and* PR descriptions during review. Active voice, omit needless words, drop parentheticals.
7. **"Comment-as-smell" heuristic from review.** When the only way to justify a special case (`_INT_ONLY_FIELDS`) is a 4-line explanatory comment, delete the special case. This is a real review heuristic that isn't in any skill I know of.
8. **Doc cross-reference discipline in code.** Reviewer said: code shouldn't reference `README §N`-style section numbers (couples two artefacts that evolve independently); pointing at `docs/design-decisions.md §N` is borderline and case-by-case. This is a comment-style rule, finer-grained than the existing "no ticket IDs in comments" rule.
9. **Fetching reviewer comments via `gh api`.** Workflow detail — `gh api repos/{owner}/{repo}/pulls/{N}/comments` was the load-bearing primitive for reading reviews.

Now I'll check Linear to see which of these already have homes.

{"id":"fdbb920e-a39c-4b83-83a4-348d2bf06df5","icon":":gear:","color":"#bec2c8","name":"Workflow governance — single source, installable on any machine","summary":"Package workflow governance as a separately-installable pack (repo agent-skills-workflow, plugin workflow) with Linear as one module — distinct from Shaper, so it travels to any machine and adopting Shaper never forces a tracker.","description":"# Initiative — workflow governance, single-sourced and installable anywhere\n\n**Goal:** For anyone who wants to work the way I do — me included, on a new machine — we want our workflow governance to be adoptable on its own, separate from the Shaper methodology, so adopting Shaper never forces a tracker and the patterns travel to any machine unchanged.\n\n## Key results\n\n**KR1 (commit)** — Governance installs and loads on a clean machine with zero per-machine config. *(bet)*\n\n* baseline: loads only via absolute-path `@`-imports in `~/.claude/CLAUDE.md`; no install path exists.\n* target: on a clean profile, installing the pack makes a new session show the GOVERNANCE index (injected by the SessionStart hook), with 0 rule `@`-imports left in `~/.claude/CLAUDE.md`.\n* measured over: by cycle close.\n* how we'll know: install on a clean Claude profile; inspect SessionStart output; `grep -n \"@\" ~/.claude/CLAUDE.md`.\n\n*Layer 1: operability · Layer 2: golden-path test*\n\n**KR2 (commit)** — Shaper carries no hard tracker dependency; initiative-shape runs tracker-free, and the Linear tracker module lives only in the new pack. *(bet)*\n\n* baseline: `rules/linear-workflow.md` lives in Shaper; `initiative-shape` Step 7 calls `mcp__claude_ai_Linear__save_project` / `save_issue` directly.\n* target: `rules/linear-workflow.md` no longer exists under `agent-skills-shaper`; `grep -rn \"mcp__claude_ai_Linear__\" agent-skills-shaper/` returns 0; `initiative-shape` STAYS in Shaper, produces a six-field markdown shape when no tracker is installed, and only hands off to a tracker \"if a binding is installed\".\n* measured over: by cycle close.\n* how we'll know: `ls agent-skills-shaper/rules/linear-workflow.md` (absent) + `grep -rn \"mcp__claude_ai_Linear__\" /Users/anton/src/agent-skills-shaper/` (= 0) + a dry run of `/initiative-shape` with no Workflow pack that emits the markdown shape rather than calling Linear.\n\n*Layer 1: correctness · Layer 2: golden-path (tracker-free run) + grep-clean*\n\n**KR3 (commit)** — Each wired repo points once at the single source and restates nothing. *(brake)*\n\n* baseline: 4 AGENTS.md restate the Linear workflow; 1 carries the \"four-field\" drift; 3 carry stale `mcp__linear-server__` names (agent-skills, nestl, stock-screener).\n* target: each in-scope AGENTS.md has one path-free pack pointer; `grep -rn \"## Linear workflow\" / \"four-field\" / \"mcp__linear-server__\"` across them = 0.\n* measured over: by cycle close.\n* how we'll know: the three greps across agent-skills-shaper, agent-skills, nestl, stock-screener all return 0.\n\n*Layer 1: discipline · Layer 2: fitness function (grep-clean)*\n\n**KR4 (commit)** — A second install at a pinned version reproduces the same governance, and the iterate loop is documented. *(foundation)*\n\n* baseline: no marketplace entry, no version, no install/iterate docs.\n* target: versioned plugin manifest + marketplace entry + README documenting install and the edit→bump→push→update loop; the pinned version reproduces the same index on a second profile.\n* measured over: by cycle close.\n* how we'll know: inspect manifest (version) + README (install + iterate sections); install pinned version on a second profile and diff the injected index.\n\n*Layer 1: maintenance · Layer 2: artefact-exists + reproduce-on-second-install*\n\n*Dimensions: operability / correctness / discipline / maintenance — all distinct (rubric rule 5b). Roles: 2 bet + 1 brake + 1 foundation.*\n\n## Affected repos\n\nNEW `agent-skills-workflow` (plugin handle `workflow`); `agent-skills-shaper`; `agent-skills`; `nestl`; `stock-screener`; + global `~/.claude` config (not a repo). **EXCLUDED:** `adyen-onboarding` (Tolaria vault, not a code project). *(*`stock-review` *was an earlier mislabel — a stray* `ui/` *build-cache dir with no git remote; the real repo is* `stock-screener`*.)*\n\n## Appetite\n\n\\~9 issues (Medium — one full cycle slot).\n\n## Kill condition\n\nThe SessionStart-hook loading proves unreliable in practice (governance fails to inject, or principle-grounding degrades). Fallback: ship the pack without the hook and load `GOVERNANCE.md` via one documented per-machine import line.","url":"https://linear.app/ababushkin/project/workflow-governance-single-source-installable-on-any-machine-6b861e6c3769","createdAt":"2026-05-25T06:09:08.731Z","updatedAt":"2026-05-25T13:38:48.394Z","startedAt":null,"completedAt":null,"canceledAt":null,"startDate":null,"startDateResolution":null,"targetDate":null,"targetDateResolution":null,"priority":{"value":2,"name":"High"},"labels":[],"initiatives":[{"id":"d06c222a-5de9-4d47-8423-f294a4dacfd8","name":"Planning overhaul with linear decoupling"}],"lead":{"id":"720d0535-dfc6-465f-8111-d1a3e6eab786","name":"Anton Babushkin"},"status":{"id":"2941e5ab-173a-4b38-8032-c21209bb8883","name":"Backlog","type":"backlog"},"teams":[{"id":"c20f56da-f121-4653-bf6c-dc1c8aac21c1","name":"Personal","key":"ABA"}]}

## Step 3 — Proposed plan

The cleanest home is the **agent-skills-workflow** pack (the scaffold exists; ABA-265–268 are Done). The amount of work is ~3 issues total — below the 5-issue threshold for a new initiative per `linear-workflow.md`, so these belong in the **ops container** ("Ops - bugs, maintenance, emergent"), not as a fresh project. Scheduled to land **after** the in-flight workflow-pack surgery (ABA-269–273) is done, so the pack's structure is stable when the module is added.

I'm proposing **two confident issues + one optional**:

**Issue 1 (agent-skills-workflow): Add `rules/stacked-pr-delivery.md` module** — captures the patterns from this run. Three sections:
- *Per-layer ritual.* `/code-review-and-quality` + commit + push happen per layer (before each `gt submit`); tracker-Done deferred until the whole stack merges; STOP-and-wait after each push.
- *Owner-authorised push-to-main exceptions.* How a per-issue exception to the repo's AGENTS.md default is recorded so it survives across sessions (one-line directive at issue start, re-asserted in any handoff prompt).
- *Review-comment triage on a stack.* `gh api repos/{owner}/{repo}/pulls/{N}/comments` to fetch; amend bottom-up; `gt restack` between layers; `gt submit --stack` to push; Edit-tool "file modified" recovery (re-read, re-edit; don't blind-retry).
- Cross-link from `rules/GOVERNANCE.md`.
- Covers diffs #2, #3, #4, #9.

**Issue 2 (agent-skills): Add two heuristics to `code-review-and-quality`** —
- *Comment-as-smell:* if the only justification for a special case is a multi-line explanatory comment, prefer deleting the special case. The `_INT_ONLY_FIELDS` example from this run is the canonical motivation.
- *Doc cross-reference discipline:* code comments don't reference README §N (couples two artefacts that evolve independently); references to `docs/design-decisions.md §N` are borderline — default to no link, accept only when the link survives a "would this still be true if the section number shifts?" sanity check.
- Covers diffs #7, #8.

**Issue 3 (optional, agent-skills or agent-skills-workflow): Wire `/writing-clearly-and-consisely` into PR-deliverable review** — single prompt in either `code-review-and-quality` (for prose-file changes) or a future PR-creation skill saying "when the diff touches README, design-decisions, or a PR body draft, pass it through `/writing-clearly-and-consisely` before submit." This is weaker — it codifies what the reviewer (you) did organically. Worth doing only if you want this to happen without explicit prompting.
- Covers diffs #5, #6.

**Not proposing anything for drain-cycle.** Its side of stacked PRs is fully shaped in ABA-300–304; the gaps from this run are workflow rules, not drain-cycle features.

## Agent-Skills-Workflow Repository Map

**Repository location:** `/Users/anton/src/agent-skills-workflow/`

### 1. Rule modules location and inventory

**Path:** `/Users/anton/src/agent-skills-workflow/rules/`

**Files present:**
- `GOVERNANCE.md` (49 lines) — master index and always-on rules
- `linear-workflow.md` (203 lines) — tracker mechanics for Linear (initiative model, cycle lifecycle, issue workflow, backlog rules)
- `code-review.md` (29 lines) — completion gate (review → fix → commit+push, tracker-agnostic)
- `.gitkeep` — directory marker

**Coverage:** Linear tracker integration, code review gate discipline, and workflow governance. No Graphite/stacked PR content currently exists.

### 2. Canonical module format: linear-workflow.md

**Typical length:** 203 lines (substantial but focused single-domain guidance)

**Top-level structure:**
- Preamble: cross-pack defer note (to Shaper pack if installed)
- 8 major sections (marked with `##`): The initiative model, Cycle model, Backlog, Issue workflow, Linear project conventions, Capturing a shaped initiative in Linear, What this model replaces
- 13 subsections (marked with `###`): Initiative size, Initiative lifecycle, Creating an initiative, Cycle composition, Cycle planning, Cycle close, On start, On completion, Blocked, New work surfaced mid-flight, Steps, After capture
- Tables, code blocks, and procedural lists

**Tone:** Prescriptive but explanatory. Written in second person ("you"). Contains normative statements ("must", "do not") with rationale footnotes.

**Shape:** The "On start / On completion" pattern appears as subsections under "Issue workflow" (lines 126–145):
- **On start:** pre-work checklist (move to In Progress, cycle assignment, delegation handling)
- **On completion:** post-work gate (review → fix → commit, summary comment, transition to Done)

**Verbatim commands:** None hardcoded as shell scripts. Instead, references to MCP tools inline: 
- `mcp__claude_ai_Linear__save_issue` (state transitions)
- `mcp__claude_ai_Linear__save_comment` (issue comments)
- `mcp__claude_ai_Linear__save_project` (project creation)
- `mcp__claude_ai_Linear__get_project` (readback verification)

### 3. GOVERNANCE.md indexing model

**Path:** `/Users/anton/src/agent-skills-workflow/rules/GOVERNANCE.md`

**How modules are cross-linked:**
GOVERNANCE.md is the SessionStart-injected "always-on" index. It contains four inline sections for short rules (completion gate, comments, Linear lifecycle, Shaper pack awareness), then **points outward** to module files:

```markdown
Read `rules/code-review.md` for the full tracker-agnostic gate and its rationale.
...
Read `rules/linear-workflow.md` for the full cycle / backlog / capture / issue-workflow rubric.
```

**Registration requirement:** To index a new module, add a `## <Module Name> — <state>` heading (e.g. `## Graphite workflow — read when working with stacked PRs`) and include either inline rules or a `Read <path>` pointer. The SessionStart hook injects only GOVERNANCE.md; modules are consulted via explicit cross-reference.

### 4. Plugin manifest and registration

**File:** `/Users/anton/src/agent-skills-workflow/.claude-plugin/plugin.json`

**Key fields:**
- `name`: "workflow"
- `hooks`: "./hooks/hooks.json"

**Hook wiring:** SessionStart hook (`hooks/session-start.sh`) injects GOVERNANCE.md as a JSON priority message at session start. No skill discovery or module registry — rules are pure Markdown files in `rules/`.

**Registration step beyond GOVERNANCE.md:** None. Adding a new module to `rules/` requires:
1. Create the `.md` file under `rules/`
2. Add a cross-reference in `GOVERNANCE.md` (either inline rule with a `Read rules/<filename>` pointer, or a new section)
3. Increment version in `plugin.json` and push to trigger user updates

### 5. Graphite / stacked PR references

**Result:** No pre-existing content. Grep search across `rules/`, `skills/`, `hooks/` found zero matches for: `graphite`, `gt submit`, `stacked`, `gh api`, `pulls/`, `restack`.

**Implication:** The new Graphite runbook is entirely new content — no existing module to extend.

### 6. Idiomatic MCP tool references

**From linear-workflow.md (lines 128–139):**
```markdown
- Move to **In Progress** via `mcp__claude_ai_Linear__save_issue`.
...
2. **Summary comment** — Post a short comment on the Linear issue via `mcp__claude_ai_Linear__save_comment` 
   with the review summary (count of findings by severity, plus what was fixed vs deferred).
3. **Done** — Transition to Done via `mcp__claude_ai_Linear__save_issue`.
```

**Style pattern:** Backtick-wrapped MCP tool names inline with imperative prose. Tool names are fully qualified (`mcp__<domain>_<service>__<operation>`). Appears in action descriptions, not as standalone code blocks.
